Chapter 1 Introduction to Probability Theory 1.1 The Binomial Asset Pricing Model The binomial asset pricing model provides a powerful tool to understand arbitrage pricing theory and probability theory. In this course, we shall use it for both these purposes. In the binomial asset pricing model, we model stock prices in discrete time, assuming that at each step, the stock price will change to one of two possible values. Let us begin with an initial positive stock price So- There are two positive numbers, d and u, with 0<d<u, (1.1) such that at the next period, the stock price will be either dSo or uSo- Typically, we take d and u to satisfy 0 < d < 1 < u, so change of the stock price from Sq to dSo represents a downward movement, and change of the stock price from Sq to uSq represents an upward movement. It is common to also have d = ^, and this will be the case in many of our examples. However, strictly speaking, for what we are about to do we need to assume only (1.1) and (1.2) below. Of course, stock price movements are much more complicated than indicated by the binomial asset pricing model. We consider this simple model for three reasons. First of all, within this model the concept of arbitrage pricing and its relation to risk-neutral pricing is clearly illuminated. Secondly, the model is used in practice because with a sufficient number of steps, it provides a good, computationally tractable approximation to continuous-time models. Thirdly, within the binomial model we can develop the theory of conditional expectations and martingales which lies at the heart of continuous-time models. With this third motivation in mind, we develop notation for the binomial model which is a bit different from that normally found in practice. Let us imagine that we are tossing a coin, and when we get a "Head," the stock price moves up, but when we get a "Tail," the price moves down. We denote the price at time 1 by S\ (H) = uSq if the toss results in head (H), and by S\ (T) = dSo if it 11

12 S2(HH) = 16 S2(HT) =4 SJTH) =4 S2(TT) = 1 Figure 1.1: Binomial tree of stock prices with Sq = 4, u = 1/d = 2. results in tail (T). After the second toss, the price will be one of: S2(HH) = uSi(H) = u2S0, S2(HT) = dSi(H) = duS0, S2 {TH) = uSi (T) = udS0, S2 {TT) = dSt (T) = d2S0. After three tosses, there are eight possible coin sequences, although not all of them result in different stock prices at time 3. For the moment, let us assume that the third toss is the last one and denote by Q = {HHH, HHT, HTH, HTT, THH, THT, TTH, TTT} the set of all possible outcomes of the three tosses. The set Q of all possible outcomes of a random experiment is called the sample space for the experiment, and the elements u of Q are called sample points. In this case, each sample point u is a sequence of length three. We denote the k-th component of toby to k- For example, when u = HTH, we have u\ = H, u>2 = T and lu3 = H. The stock price Sk at time k depends on the coin tosses. To emphasize this, we often write Sk(oj). Actually, this notation does not quite tell the whole story, for while S3 depends on all of u, S2 depends on only the first two components of u, S\ depends on only the first component of u, and 5*0 does not depend on u at all. Sometimes we will use notation such S2 (a>i, u2) just to record more explicitly how S2 depends onw= (u\, lo2 , w3). Example 1.1 Set So = 4, u = 2 and d = \. We have then the binomial "tree" of possible stock prices shown in Fig. 1.1. Each sample point u = (u\,u2,uz) represents a path through the tree. Thus, we can think of the sample space Q as either the set of all possible outcomes from three coin tosses or as the set of all possible paths through the tree. To complete our binomial asset pricing model, we introduce a money market with interest rate r; $1 invested in the money market becomes $(1 + r) in the next period. We take r to be the interest .Sj(H):

CHAPTER 1. Introduction to Probability Theory 13 rate for both borrowing and lending. (This is not as ridiculous as it first seems, because in a many applications of the model, an agent is either borrowing or lending (not both) and knows in advance which she will be doing; in such an application, she should take r to be the rate of interest for her activity.) We assume that d < 1 + r < u. (1.2) The model would not make sense if we did not have this condition. For example, if 1 + r > u, then the rate of return on the money market is always at least as great as and sometimes greater than the return on the stock, and no one would invest in the stock. The inequality d > 1 + r cannot happen unless either r is negative (which never happens, except maybe once upon a time in Switzerland) or d > 1. In the latter case, the stock does not really go "down" if we get a tail; it just goes up less than if we had gotten a head. One should borrow money at interest rate r and invest in the stock, since even in the worst case, the stock price rises at least as fast as the debt used to buy it. With the stock as the underlying asset, let us consider a European call option with strike price K > 0 and expiration time 1. This option confers the right to buy the stock at time 1 for K dollars, and so is worth S\ — K at time 1 if S\ — K is positive and is otherwise worth zero. We denote by Vi(w) = (Siiu) - K)+ = max{1S,i(w) - K, 0} the value (payoff) of this option at expiration. Of course, V\(u) actually depends only on u\, and we can and do sometimes write V\ (u\) rather than V\ (to). Our first task is to compute the arbitrage price of this option at time zero. Suppose at time zero you sell the call for Vb dollars, where Vb is still to be determined. You now have an obligation to pay off (uSo — K)+ if u\ = H and to pay off (dSo — K)+ if u\ = T. At the time you sell the option, you don't yet know which value u\ will take. You hedge your short position in the option by buying A0 shares of stock, where A0 is still to be determined. You can use the proceeds Vb of the sale of the option for this purpose, and then borrow if necessary at interest rate r to complete the purchase. If Vb is more than necessary to buy the A0 shares of stock, you invest the residual money at interest rate r. In either case, you will have Vb — AoSb dollars invested in the money market, where this quantity might be negative. You will also own A0 shares of stock. If the stock goes up, the value of your portfolio (excluding the short position in the option) is A0,Si(H) + (l + r)(Vb-A0,So), and you need to have V\ (H). Thus, you want to choose Vb and A0 so that Vi(ff) = AoSi(ff) + (1 + r)(V0 - A0S0). (1.3) If the stock goes down, the value of your portfolio is A0,Si(T) + (l + r)(Vb-A0,So), and you need to have V\ (T). Thus, you want to choose Vb and A0 to also have Vi(T) = AoSi(r) + (1 + r)(Vo - A0S0). (1.4)

This formula is so pervasive the when a practitioner says "delta", she means the derivative (in the sense of calculus) just described. Depending on how uncertainty enters the model, there can be cases in which the number of shares of stock a hedge should hold is not the (calculus) derivative of the derivative security with respect to the price of the underlying asset. Introduction to Probability Theory 17 Definition 1.1 A probability measure IP is a function mapping T into [0,1] with the following properties: (i) P{Q) = 1, (ii) If A\, A2l ■ ■ ■ is a sequence of disjoint sets in T, then / oo \ oo F(\jAk)='£F(Ak). \k=l / fc=l Probability measures have the following interpretation. Let A be a subset of T. Imagine that Q is the set of all possible outcomes of some random experiment. There is a certain probability, between 0 and 1, that when that experiment is performed, the outcome will lie in the set A. We think of P(A) as this probability. As in the above example, it is generally the case that we specify a probability measure on only some of the subsets of Q and then use property (ii) of Definition 1.1 to determine P{ A) for the remaining sets A G T. Definition 1.2 Let Q be a nonempty set. A u-algebra is a collection Q of subsets of Q with the following three properties: (i) 0 G Q,

18 (ii) If A £ Q, then its complement Ac £ Q, (iii) If Ai, A2l A3,... is a sequence of sets in Q, then U^l1 Ak is also in Q. Here are some important u-algebras of subsets of the set Q in Example 1.2: J"i = h,Q, {HHH, HHT, HTH, HTT}, {THH,THT,TTH,TTT}\, T2 = h,tt, {HHH, HHT}, {HTH, HTT}, {THH, THT}, {TTH, TTT}, and all sets which can be built by taking unions of these >, Tz = T = The set of all subsets of Q. To simplify notation a bit, let us define AH = {HHH, HHT, HTH, HTT} = {H on the first toss}, so that and let us define AT = {THH, THT, TTH, TTT} = {T on the first toss}, F1 = {®,n,AH,AT}, AHH = {HHH, HHT} = {HH on the first two tosses}, AHT = {HTH, HTT} = {HT on the first two tosses}, ATH = {THH, THT} = {TH on the first two tosses}, ATT = {TTH, TTT} = {TT on the first two tosses}, so that ?2 = {9,n,AHH,AHT,ATH,ATT, Ah, At, Ahh U Ath, Ahh U Att, Aht U Ath, Aht U Att, Achhi Achti Athi Att}- We interpret u-algebras as a record of information. Suppose the coin is tossed three times, and you are not told the outcome, but you are told, for every set in T\ whether or not the outcome is in that set. For example, you would be told that the outcome is not in 0 and is in Q. Moreover, you might be told that the outcome is not in Ajj but is in At- In effect, you have been told that the first toss was a T, and nothing more. The u-algebra T\ is said to contain the "information of the first toss", which is usually called the "information up to time 1". Similarly, T2 contains the "information of

CHAPTER 1. Introduction to Probability Theory 19 the first two tosses," which is the "information up to time 2." The u-algebra T-$ = T contains "full information" about the outcome of all three tosses. The so-called "trivial" u-algebra T§ contains no information. Knowing whether the outcome u of the three tosses is in 0 (it is not) and whether it is in Q (it is) tells you nothing about u Definition 1.3 Let Q be a nonempty finite set. A filtration is a sequence of u-algebras Tq , T\, T2, • • •, T,, such that each u-algebra in the sequence contains all the sets contained by the previous u-algebra. Definition 1.4 Let Q be a nonempty finite set and let T be the u-algebra of all subsets of Q. A random variable is a function mapping Q into M. Example 1.3 Let Q be given by (2.1) and consider the binomial asset pricing Example 1.1, where S0 = 4, u = 2 and d = \. Then So, S\, S2 and S3 are all random variables. For example, S2{HHT) = u2So = 16. The "random variable" So is really not random, since So(co) = 4 for all to <E Q. Nonetheless, it is a function mapping Q into M, and thus technically a random variable, albeit a degenerate one. A random variable maps Q into M, and we can look at the preimage under the random variable of sets in M. Consider, for example, the random variable S2 of Example 1.1. We have S2{HHH) = S2{HHT) = 16, S2(HTH) = S2(HTT) = S2(THH) = S2(THT) = 4, S2(TTH) = S2(TTT) = 1. Let us consider the interval [4, 27]. The preimage under S2 of this interval is defined to be {oj e tt; S2(u) e [4, 27]} = {u eQ;4< S2 < 27} = ACTT. The complete list of subsets of Q we can get as preimages of sets in M is: 0, Q, AHH, AHT U ATH, ATT, and sets which can be built by taking unions of these. This collection of sets is a cr-algebra, called the a-algebra generated by the random variable S2, and is denoted by (t(,S,2). The information content of this cr-algebra is exactly the information learned by observing 5*2. More specifically, suppose the coin is tossed three times and you do not know the outcome u, but someone is willing to tell you, for each set in ^(Sy, whether u is in the set. You might be told, for example, that u is not in Ahh, is in Aht U Ath, and is not in Att- Then you know that in the first two tosses, there was a head and a tail, and you know nothing more. This information is the same you would have gotten by being told that the value of S2 (to) is 4. Note that T2 defined earlier contains all the sets which are in ^(Sy, and even more. This means that the information in the first two tosses is greater than the information in S2. In particular, if you see the first two tosses, you can distinguish Aht from Ath, but you cannot make this distinction from knowing the value of S2 alone.

By the distribution of a random variable X, we mean any of the several ways of characterizing jCx ■ If X is discrete, as in the case of ,S*2 above, we can either tell where the masses are and how large they are, or tell what the cumulative distribution function is. In order to work through the concept of a risk-neutral measure, we set up the definitions to make a clear distinction between random variables and their distributions. A random variable is a mapping from Q to M, nothing more. It has an existence quite apart from discussion of probabilities. For example, in the discussion above, S2(TTH) = S2(TTT) = 1, regardless of whether the probability for H is ^ or \.

CHAPTER 1. Introduction to Probability Theory 21 The distribution of a random variable is a measure Cx on M, i.e., a way of assigning probabilities to sets in M. It depends on the random variable X and the probability measure P we use in Q. If we set the probability of if to be |, then Cs2 assigns mass | to the number 16. If we set the probability of H to be ^, then Cs2 assigns mass | to the number 16. The distribution of S2 has changed, but the random variable has not. It is still defined by S2{HHH) = S2{HHT) = 16, S2(HTH) = S2(HTT) = S2(THH) = S2(THT) = 4, S2(TTH) = S2(TTT) = 1. Thus, a random variable can have more than one distribution (a "market" or "objective" distribution, and a "risk-neutral" distribution). In a similar vein, two different random variables can have the same distribution. Suppose in the binomial model of Example 1.1, the probability of H and the probability of T is \. Consider a European call with strike price 14 expiring at time 2. The payoff of the call at time 2 is the random variable {S2 — 14)+, which takes the value 2 if u = HHH or u = HHT, and takes the value 0 in every other case. The probability the payoff is 2 is |, and the probability it is zero is |. Consider also a European put with strike price 3 expiring at time 2. The payoff of the put at time 2 is (3 — S2)+, which takes the value 2 if u = TTH or u = TTT. Like the payoff of the call, the payoff of the put is 2 with probability | and 0 with probability |. The payoffs of the call and the put are different random variables having the same distribution. We define the Lebesgue measure of intervals in P to be their length. This definition and the properties of measure determine the Lebesgue measure of many, but not all, subsets of P. The collection of subsets of P we consider, and for which Lebesgue measure is defined, is the collection of Borel sets defined below. We use Lebesgue measure to construct the Lebesgue integral, a generalization of the Riemann integral. We need this integral because, unlike the Riemann integral, it can be defined on abstract spaces, such as the space of infinite sequences of coin tosses or the space of paths of Brownian motion. This section concerns the Lebesgue integral on the space P only; the generalization to other spaces will be given later.

CHAPTER 1. Introduction to Probability Theory 23 Definition 1.9 The Borel a-algebra, denoted B{M), is the smallest u-algebra containing all open intervals in M. The sets in B{M) are called Borel sets. Every set which can be written down and just about every set imaginable is in B{M). The following discussion of this fact uses the cr-algebra properties developed in Problem 1.3. By definition, every open interval (a, b) is in B{M), where a and b are real numbers. Since B{M) is a (T-algebra, every union of open intervals is also in B{M). For example, for every real number a, the open half-line oo (a, oo) = M (a, a + n) n=l is a Borel set, as is oo ( — oo, a) = M (a — n, a). n=l For real numbers a and b, the union ( —oo, a) U (b, oo) is Borel. Since B{M) is a u-algebra, every complement of a Borel set is Borel, so B{M) contains [a, b] = (( — oo, a) U (6, oo) j . This shows that every closed interval is Borel. In addition, the closed half-lines oo [a, oo) = M [a, a + n] n=l and oo ( —oo, a] = M [a — n, a] n=l are Borel. Half-open and half-closed intervals are also Borel, since they can be written as intersections of open half-lines and closed half-lines. For example, (a, b] = ( —oo, b] fl (a, oo). Every set which contains only one real number is Borel. Indeed, if a is a real number, then {a} = P| [a , a+ - ) . n=l This means that every set containing finitely many real numbers is Borel; if A = {ai, a2,..., an}, then n a= UKI- k=l

24 In fact, every set containing countably infinitely many numbers is Borel; if A = {ai, a2,...}, then n a= UKI- fc=l This means that the set of rational numbers is Borel, as is its complement, the set of irrational numbers. There are, however, sets which are not Borel. We have just seen that any non-Borel set must have uncountably many points. Example 1.4 (The Cantor set.) We may not compute the Lebesgue measure of an uncountable set by adding up the Lebesgue measure of its individual members, because there is no way to add up uncountably many numbers. The integral was invented to get around this problem. In order to think about Lebesgue integrals, we must first consider the functions to be integrated. 32 Example 1.10 Infinite coin toss space. Toss a coin repeatedly without stopping, so that Q is the set of all nonterminating sequences of H and T. We call this space Q^. This is an uncountably infinite space, and we need to exercise some care in the construction of the cr-algebra we will use here. For each positive integer n, we define Tn to be the u-algebra determined by the first n tosses. For example, T2 contains four basic sets, Ahh = W={(jJi,(jJ2,u3,...);(jj1 = H,(jj2 = H} = The set of all sequences which begin with HH, AHt = W={(jJi,(jJ2,u3,...);(jJ1 = H,(jj2 = T} = The set of all sequences which begin with HT, ATH = W={(jJi,(jJ2,u3,...);(jj1 = T,(jj2 = H} = The set of all sequences which begin with TH, ATT = {u= (uuu2,u3n...);ul = T,u2 = T} = The set of all sequences which begin with TT. Because T2 is a u-algebra, we must also put into it the sets 0, Q, and all unions of the four basic sets. In the (T-algebra T, we put every set in every u-algebra Tn, where n ranges over the positive integers. We also put in every other set which is required to make T be a u-algebra. For example, the set containing the single sequence {HHHHH ■ ■ ■} = {H on every toss} is not in any of the Tn u-algebras, because it depends on all the components of the sequence and not just the first n components. However, for each positive integer n, the set {if on the first n tosses} is in Tn and hence in T. Therefore, 00 {if on every toss} = P| {H on the first n tosses} n=l is also in T. We next construct the probability measure P on (f^oo, F) which corresponds to probability p £ [0,1] for H and probability q = 1 — p for T. Let A £ T be given. If there is a positive integer n such that A £ Tn, then the description of A depends on only the first n tosses, and it is clear how to define P{A). For example, suppose A = Ahh U Ath, where these sets were defined earlier. Then A is in T2. We set P{Ahh) = P2 and IP(Ath) = QP, and then we have P{A) = F(AHH U ATH) =p2 + qp=(p+ q)p = p. In other words, the probability of a H on the second toss is p.

CHAPTER 1. Introduction to Probability Theory 33 Let us now consider a set A £ T for which there is no positive integer n such that A £ T. Such is the case for the set {H on every toss}. To determine the probability of these sets, we write them in terms of sets which are in Tn for positive integers n, and then use the properties of probability measures listed in Remark 1.1. For example, {if on the first toss} D {H on the first two tosses} D {H on the first three tosses} 5 ■■■, and oo Pi {H on the first n tosses} = {H on every toss}. n=l According to Remark 1.1(d) (continuity from above), P{H on every toss} = lim P{H on the first n tosses} = lim pn. n—>oo n—>oo If p = 1, then P{H on every toss} = 1; otherwise, P{H on every toss} = 0. A similar argument shows that if 0 < p < 1 so that 0 < q < 1, then every set in Q^ which contains only one element (nonterminating sequence of H and T) has probability zero, and hence very set which contains countably many elements also has probabiliy zero. We are in a case very similar to Lebesgue measure: every point has measure zero, but sets can have positive measure. Of course, the only sets which can have positive probabilty in Q^ are those which contain uncountably many elements. R 1.5 Independence In this section, we define and discuss the notion of independence in a general probability space (S7, T', P), although most of the examples we give will be for coin toss space. 1.5.1 Independence of sets Definition 1.15 We say that two sets A £ T and B £ T are independent if P(AHB) = P(A)P(B). Suppose a random experiment is conducted, and u is the outcome. The probability that u £ A is P{A). Suppose you are not told u, but you are told that u £ B. Conditional on this information, the probability that u £ A is V ' ; P(B) The sets A and B are independent if and only if this conditional probability is the uncondidtional probability P(A), i.e., knowing that u £ B does not change the probability you assign to A. This discussion is symmetric with respect to A and B; if A and B are independent and you know that to £ A, the conditional probability you assign to B is still the unconditional probability P{B). Whether two sets are independent depends on the probability measure P. For example, suppose we toss a coin twice, with probability p for H and probability q = 1 — p for T on each toss. To avoid trivialities, we assume that 0 < p < 1. Then P{HH} = p2, P{HT} = P{TH} = pq, P{TT} = q2. (5.1)

CHAPTER 1. Introduction to Probability Theory 41 Let A = {HH, HT} and B = {HT, TH}. In words, A is the set "H on the first toss" and B is the set "one H and one T." Then An B = {HT}. We compute P{A) = p + pq = p, ^(5) = 2pg, 1P(A)1P(B) = 2p2q, P(A DB) = pq. These sets are independent if and only if 2p2q = pq, which is the case if and only if p = \. If p = ^, then P{B), the probability of one head and one tail, is \. If you are told that the coin tosses resulted in a head on the first toss, the probability of B, which is now the probability of a T on the second toss, is still \. Suppose however that p = 0.01. By far the most likely outcome of the two coin tosses is TT, and the probability of one head and one tail is quite small; in fact, P{B) = 0.0198. However, if you are told that the first toss resulted in H, it becomes very likely that the two tosses result in one head and one tail. In fact, conditioned on getting a if on the first toss, the probability of one H and one T is the probability of a T on the second toss, which is 0.99. 1.5.2 Independence of cr-algebras Definition 1.16 Let Q and % be sub-u-algebras of T. We say that Q and % are independent if every set in Q is independent of every set in H, i.e, P(A f\B) = P(A)P(B) for every AeTl, B G Q. Example 1.14 Toss a coin twice, and let P be given by (5.1). Let Q = T\ be the u-algebra determined by the first toss: Q contains the sets 0, Q, {HH, HT}, {TH, TT}. Let % be the u-albegra determined by the second toss: % contains the sets 0, Q, {HH, TH}, {HT, TT}. These two u-algebras are independent. For example, if we choose the set {HH, HT} from Q and the set {HH, TH} from Tt, then we have P{HH, HT}P{HH, TH}= (p2 + pq) (p2 + pq) = p2, P({HH, HT} n {HH, TH}) = P{HH} = p2. No matter which set we choose in Q and which set we choose in %, we will find that the product of the probabilties is the probability of the intersection.

42 Example 1.14 illustrates the general principle that when the probability for a sequence of tosses is defined to be the product of the probabilities for the individual tosses of the sequence, then every set depending on a particular toss will be independent of every set depending on a different toss. We say that the different tosses are independent when we construct probabilities this way. It is also possible to construct probabilities such that the different tosses are not independent, as shown by the following example. Example 1.15 Define P for the individual elements of tt = {HH, HT, TH, TT} to be P{HH} = l-, P{HT} = 2-, P{TH} = i, P{TT} = i, and for every set A C Q, define P{ A) to be the sum of the probabilities of the elements in A. Then P(Q) = 1, so P is a probability measure. Note that the sets {H on first toss} = {HH, HT} and {H on second toss} = {HH, TH} have probabilities P{HH, HT} = ± and P{HH, TH} = |, so the product of the probabilities is ^. On the other hand, the intersection of {HH, HT} and {HH, TH} contains the single element {HH}, which has probability |. These sets are not independent. 1.5.3 Independence of random variables Definition 1.17 We say that two random variables X and Y are independent if the u-algebras they generate a(X) and a(Y) are independent. In the probability space of three independent coin tosses, the price S2 of the stock at time 2 is independent of J3-. This is because S2 depends on only the first two coin tosses, whereas J3- is either u or d, depending on whether the third coin toss is H or T. Definition 1.17 says that for independent random variables X and Y, every set defined in terms of X is independent of every set defined in terms of Y. In the case of S2 and -^ just considered, for example, the sets {S2 = udS0} = {HTH, HTT} and {g- = w} = {HHH, HTH, THH, TTH} are indepedent sets. Suppose X and Y are independent random variables. We defined earlier the measure induced by X on P to be CX{A) = P{X eA}, AC P. Similarly, the measure induced by Y is CY{B) = P{Y e B}, B C P. Now the pair [X, Y) takes values in the plane P2, and we can define the measure induced by the pair Cx,y{C) = P{{X,Y) eC}, CCP2. The set C in this last equation is a subset of the plane P2. In particular, C could be a "rectangle", i.e, a set of the form Ax B, where AC P and B C P. In this case, {{X, Y)eAxB} = {XeA}n {Y e B},

Theorem 5.7 Suppose X and Y are independent random variables. Let g and h be functions from M to M. Then g(X) and h(Y) are also independent random variables. Proof: Let us denote W = g{X) and Z = h(Y). We must consider sets in a(W) and o{Z). But a typical set in a(W) is of the form {u;W(u) e A} = {u : g(X(u)) e A}, which is defined in terms of the random variable X. Therefore, this set is in (t(X). (In general, we have that every set in a(W) is also in (t(X), which means that X contains at least as much information as W. In fact, X can contain strictly more information than W, which means that a(X) will contain all the sets in a(W) and others besides; this is the case, for example, HW = X2.) In the same way that we just argued that every set in a(W) is also in &(X), we can show that every set in o{Z) is also in cr(Y). Since every set in a(X) is independent of every set in o{Y), we conclude that every set in a (W) is independent of every set ina(Z). o

Example 1.16 Let X be a standard normal random variable, let Z be independent of X and have the distribution P{Z = 1} = P{Z = -1} = 0. Define Y = XZ. If X and Y were independent, so would be X2 and Y2, but in fact, X2 = Y2 almost surely.

We are not going to give the proof of this theorem, but here is an argument which makes it plausible. We will use this argument later when developing stochastic calculus. The Central Limit Theorem asserts that as n —> oo, the distribution of Zn approaches that of a normal random variable with mean (expected value) zero and variance a2. Chapter 2 Conditional Expectation Please see Hull's book (Section 9.6.) 2.1 A Binomial Model for Stock Price Dynamics Stock prices are assumed to follow this simple binomial model: The initial stock price during the period under study is denoted So- At each time step, the stock price either goes up by a factor of u or down by a factor of d. It will be useful to visualize tossing a coin at each time step, and say that • the stock price moves up by a factor of u if the coin comes out heads (H), and • down by a factor of d if it comes out tails (T). Note that we are not specifying the probability of heads here. Consider a sequence of 3 tosses of the coin (See Fig. 2.1) The collection of all possible outcomes (i.e. sequences of tosses of length 3) is Q = {HHH, HHT, HTH, HTT, THH, THH, THT, TTH, TTT}. A typical sequence of Q will be denoted u, and u>k will denote the kth element in the sequence u. We write Sk(oj) to denote the stock price at "time" k (i.e. after k tosses) under the outcome u. 54 (b) For every set A £ 6, we have the "partial averaging property" I YdP = I XdP. J A J A Existence. There is always a random variable Y satisfying the above properties (provided that ^|X| < oo), i.e., conditional expectations always exist. Uniqueness. There can be more than one random variable Y satisfying the above properties, but if Y' is another one, then Y = Y' almost surely, i.e., IP {to £ Q; Y(u) = Y'(oj)} = 1. Notation 2.1 For random variables X, Y, it is standard notation to write E{X\Y) = E{X\a{Y)). Here are some useful ways to think about E(X\Q): • A random experiment is performed, i.e., an element u of Q is selected. The value of u is partially but not fully revealed to us, and thus we cannot compute the exact value of X(u). Based on what we know about u, we compute an estimate of X(u). Because this estimate depends on the partial information we have about u, it depends on u, i.e., 1?[X|Y](cj) is a function of u, although the dependence on u is often not shown explicitly. • If the (T-algebra Q contains finitely many sets, there will be a "smallest" set A in Q containing u, which is the intersection of all sets in Q containing u. The way u is partially revealed to us is that we are told it is in A, but not told which element of A it is. We then define E[X \Y] (to) to be the average (with respect to IP) value of X over this set A. Thus, for all u in this set A, E[X\Y] (w) will be the same. 2.3.3 Further discussion of Partial Averaging The partial averaging property is f E(X\g)dP= f XdP,\/AeG. (3.4) 2.3.4 Properties of Conditional Expectation Please see Willams p. 88. Proof sketches of some of the properties are provided below. (a) E{E{X\G)) = E{X). Proof: Just take A in the partial averaging property to be Q. The conditional expectation of X is thus an unbiased estimator of the random variable X. (b) If X is (/-measurable, then E{X\Q) = X. Proof: The partial averaging property holds trivially when Y is replaced by X. And since X is (/-measurable, X satisfies the requirement (a) of a conditional expectation as well. If the information content of Q is sufficient to determine X, then the best estimate of X based on Q is X itself. (c) (Linearity) E{alXl + a2X2\G) = axE{Xx\Q) + a2E{X2\G). (d) (Positivity) If X > 0 almost surely, then E(X\G) > 0. Proof: Take A = {to £ Q; ]E(X\Q)(u) < 0}. This set is in Q since E{X\G) is (/-measurable. Partial averaging implies JA E(X\G)dP = JA XdP. The right-hand side is greater than or equal to zero, and the left-hand side is strictly negative, unless IP (A) = 0. Therefore, P{A) = 0.

58 2.4 Martingales The ingredients are: • A probability space (Q, T, IP). • A sequence of u-algebras T$,T\,... , Tn, with the property that Tq C T\ C ... C Tn C T. Such a sequence of u-algebras is called a filtration. • A sequence of random variables M0, Mi,... , Mn. This is called a stochastic process. Conditions for a martingale: 1. Each Mk is J^^-measurable. If you know the information in Tk, then you know the value of Mfc. We say that the process {M^} is adapted to the filtration {J^fc}- 2. For each k, E(Mk+\\Tk) = M&. Martingales tend to go neither up nor down. A supermartingale tends to go down, i.e. the second condition above is replaced by E(Mk+\ \ Tk) < M^; a submartingale tends to go w/?, i.e. ^(Mfc+il^jt) > M^. Example 2.3 (Example from the binomial model.) For k = 1, 2 we already showed that E{Sk+i\Tk) = (pu + qd)Sk. For k = 0, we set JF0 = {</>, il}, the "trivial cr-algebra". Tliis cr-algebra contains no information, and any To -measurable random variable must be constant (nonrandom). Tlierefore, by definition, E(Si \To) is that constant which satisfies the averaging property / E{Sl\To)dP = f SidlP. Jn Jn The right hand side is lESi = (pu + qd)So, and so we have E{S1\T0) = (Pu + qd)So. In conclusion, • If (pu + qd) = 1 then {Sk,Tk',k = 0,1, 2, 3} is a martingale. • If (pu + qd) > 1 then {Sk,Tk',k = 0,1, 2, 3} is a submartingale. • If (pu + qd) < 1 then {Sk,Tk',k = 0,1, 2, 3} is a supermartingale.

For period k, we must solve 2k equations of the form Vk{ui,... ,uk) = ——\pVk+i{ui,... ,u)k,H) + qVk+i(u)i,... ,uk,T)]. For example, a three-month option has 66 trading days. If each day is taken to be one period, then n = 66 and 266 ~ 7 X 1019. There are three possible ways to deal with this problem: 1. Simulation. We have, for example, that V0 = (1 + r)-nEVn, and so we could compute Vb by simulation. More specifically, we could simulate n coin tosses to = (cji, ... ,un) under the risk-neutral probability measure. We could store the value of Vn(oj). We could repeat this several times and take the average value of Vn as an approximation to EVn. 2. Approximate a many-period model by a continuous-time model. Then we can use calculus and partial differential equations. We'll get to that. 3. Look for Markov structure. Example 4.2 has this. In period 2, the option in Example 4.2 has three possible values u2 (16), u2(4), v2(l), rather than four possible values V2(H H), V2(HT), V2(TH), V2(TT). If there were 66 periods, then in period 66 there would be 67 possible stock price values (since the final price depends only on the number of up-ticks of the stock price - i.e., heads - so far) and hence only 67 possible option values, rather than 266 ~ 7 X 1019.

70 4.3 Markov Processes Technical condition always present: We consider only functions on R and subsets of R which are Borel-measurable, i.e., we only consider subsets A of R that are in B and functions g : R^-R such that g~x is a function B—±B. Definition 4.1 () Let (£1, T, P) be a probability space. Let {-^/J/Lo be a filtration under T. Let {Xk}^_0 be a stochastic process on (S7, T, P). This process is said to be Markov if: • The stochastic process {Xk} is adapted to the filtration {Tk}, and • (The Markov Property). For each k = 0,1,... , n — 1, the distribution of X^+i conditioned on J^fc is the same as the distribution of Xk+i conditioned on Xk. 4.3.1 Different ways to write the Markov property (a) (Agreement of distributions). For every A G B = B(R), we have P(Xk+leA\Tk) = E[IA(Xk+l)\Tk] = E[IA(Xk+1)\Xk] = P[xk+l e A\xk]. (b) (Agreement of expectations of all functions). For every (Borel-measurable) function h : M^M for which E\h(Xk+\)\ < oo, we have E[h(Xk+l)\Tk] = E[h(Xk+l)\Xk}. (c) (Agreement of Laplace transforms.) For every u G M for which Ee k+x < oo, we have euXk+i Tk = E euXk+i xk (If we fix u and define h(x) = eux, then the equations in (b) and (c) are the same. However in (b) we have a condition which holds for every function h, and in (c) we assume this condition only for functions h of the form h( x) = eux. A main result in the theory of Laplace transforms is that if the equation holds for every h of this special form, then it holds for every h, i.e., (c) implies (b).) (d) (Agreement of characteristic functions) For every u G R, we have E [eiuXk+l\T^ = E [e^+ilXfc] , where i = y/^1. (Since |emx\ = | cos x + sin x\ < 1 we don't need to assume that E\etux\ < oo.)

CHAPTER 4. The Markov Property 71 Remark 4.1 In every case of the Markov properties where E[... \Xk] appears, we could just as well write g (Xk) for some function g. For example, form (a) of the Markov property can be restated as: For every A G B, we have P{Xk+1 eA\Tk) = g{Xk)1 where g is a function that depends on the set A. Conditions (a)-(d) are equivalent. The Markov property as stated in (a)-(d) involves the process at a "current" time k and one future time k + 1. Conditions (a)-(d) are also equivalent to conditions involving the process at time k and multiple future times. We write these apparently stronger but actually equivalent conditions below. Consequences of the Markov property. Let j be a positive integer. (A) For every Ak+\ C M,... , Ak+j C M, P[Xk+i G Ak+i, • • • , Xk+j G Ak+j\Tk\ = P[Xk+\ G Ak+i,... , Xk+j G Ak+j\Xk]. (B) For every function h : 1RJ^-1R for which ]E\h(Xk+i, ■ ■ ■ , Xk+j) | < oo, we have E[h(Xk+i,... ,Xk+j)\Tk] = E[h(Xk+i,... ,Xk+j)\Xk]. 74 • X is independent of Q; • Y is Q-measurable. Let f(x,y) be a function of two variables, and define g(y) = Ef(X,y). Then E[f(X,Y)\g] = g(Y). Remark. Example 4.5 (Showing the stock price process is Markov) Consider an n-period binomial model. Fix a time k and define X = q+1 and Q = Tk- Then X = u if Wk+i = H and X = d if Wk+i = T. Indeed, if we condition both sides of the above equation on cr(Sk) and use the tower property on the left and the fact that the right hand side is a (Sk) -measurable, we obtain E[h{Sk+i)\Sk] = ph(uSk) + qh(dSk). Thus E[h(Sk+i)\Tk] and E[h(Sk+i)\Xk] are equal and form (b) of the Markov property is proved. Chapter 6 Properties of American Derivative Securities 6.1 The properties Definition 6.1 An American derivative security is a sequence of non-negative random variables {Gk}%=0 such that each Gk is Tk-measurable. A compound European derivative security consists of n + 1 different simple European derivative securities (with the same underlying stock) expiring at times 0,1,... , n; the security that expires at time j has payoff Cj. Thus a compound European derivative security is specified by the process {Cj}j_0, where each Cj is T3--measurable, i.e., the process {C.,}"_0 is adapted to the filtration Hedging a short position (one payment). Here is how we can hedge a short position in the j'th European derivative security. The value of European derivative security j at time k is given by VkU) = (1 + r)kE[{\ + r)-iCj\Tkl k = 0,...,j, and the hedging portfolio for that security is given by A^(wi,... ,uk) = —jf— 7^— -,k = 0,... ,j - 1. Thus, starting with wealth V0 , and using the portfolio (Aq ,... , A^-_1), we can ensure that at time j we have wealth Cj. Hedging a short position (all payments). Superpose the hedges for the individual payments. In other words, start with wealth Vq = Yl]=o ^o ■ At each time k £ {0,1,... , n — 1}, first make the payment Ck and then use the portfolio Ak = Ak(k+1) + Ak(k+2) + ... + AkW

CHAPTER 6. Properties of American Derivative Securities 89 corresponding to all future payments. At the final time n, after making the final payment Cn, we will have exactly zero wealth. Suppose you own a compound European derivative security{C.,}"_0. Compute Vb n E^'K 1=0 = E n Ea + '^Q j=° and the hedging portfolio is {Afc}^~0. You can borrow Vb and consume it immediately. This leaves you with wealth X0 = —Vq. In each period k, receive the payment Ck and then use the portfolio —Afc. At the final time n, after receiving the last payment Cn, your wealth will reach zero, i.e., you will no longer have a debt. 6.4 Optimal Exercise of American Derivative Security In this section we derive the optimal exercise time for the owner of an American derivative security. Let {Gk}%=0 be an American derivative security. Let r be the stopping time the owner plans to use. (We assume that each Gk is non-negative, so we may assume without loss of generality that the owner stops at expiration - time n- if not before). Using the stopping time r, in period j the owner will receive the payment Ci \Gi. In other words, once he chooses a stopping time, the owner has effectively converted the American derivative security into a compound European derivative security, whose value is Vr M E E 3=0 J2(l + r)-n{T=j}G3 j=o = E[(l + r)-TGT]. The owner of the American derivative security can borrow this amount of money immediately, if he chooses, and invest in the market so as to exaclty pay off his debt as the payments {C.,}"_0 are received. Thus, his optimal behavior is to use a stopping time r which maximizes V0 (t) Lemma 4.22 Vq is maximized by the stopping time t* = mm{k;Vk = Gk}. Chapter 9 Pricing in terms of Market Probabilities: The Radon-Nikodym Theorem. 9.1 Radon-Nikodym Theorem Theorem 1.27 (Radon-Nikodym) Let P and P be two probability measures on a space (Q, T). Assume that for every A £ J7 satisfying P(A) = 0, we also have IP (A) = 0. Then we say that P is absolutely continuous with respect to P. Under this assumption, there is a nonegative random variable Z such that P(A) = i ZdP, MA G T, (1.1) J A and Z is called the Radon-Nikodym derivative ofP with respect to P. Remark 9.1 Equation (1.1) implies the apparently stronger condition EX = E[XZ] for every random variable X for which E\XZ\ < oo. Remark 9.2 If P is absolutely continuous with respect to P, and P is absolutely continuous with respect to P, we say that P and P are equivalent. P and P are equivalent if and only if P{A) = 0 exactly when P(A) = 0, MA G T. If P and P are equivalent and Z is the Radon-Nikodym derivative of P w.r.t. P, then -^ is the Radon-Nikodym derivative of P w.r.t. P, i.e., EX = E[XZ] MX, (1.2) EY = E[Y-h VY- (1-3) Z (Let X and Y be related by the equation Y = XZ to see that (1.2) and (1.3) are the same.) 118 9.5 Another Applicaton of the Radon-Nikodym Theorem Let (Q, T, Q) be a probability space. Let Q be a sub-u-algebra of T, and let X be a non-negative random variable with Jq X dQ = 1. We construct the conditional expectation (under Q) of X given Q. On (/, define two probability measures P{A)=Q{A) MAeG; p(A) = [ XdQ MA G a. Whenever Y is a (/-measurable random variable, we have I Y dP = f Y dQ; m Jn if Y = 1a for some A <E Q, this is just the definition of IP, and the rest follows from the "standard machine". If A G Q and F(A) = 0, then Q (A) = 0,so P{A) = 0. In other words, the measure P is absolutely continuous with respect to the measure P. The Radon-Nikodym theorem implies that there exists a (/-measurable random variable Z such that p(A) = [ z dP VAeG, Ja i.e., f X dQ = I Z dP MA G Q. Ja Ja This shows that Z has the "partial averaging" property, and since Z is (/-measurable, it is the conditional expectation (under the probability measure Q) of X given Q. The existence of conditional expectations is a consequence of the Radon-Nikodym theorem.

This says that quadratic variation for Brownian motion accumulates at rate 1 at all times along almost every path.

Chapter 15 Ito's Formula 15.1 Ito's formula for one Brownian motion We want a rule to "differentiate" expressions of the form f(B(t)), where f(x) is a differentiable function. If B (t) were also differentiable, then the ordinary chain rule would give ±f(B(t)) = f'(B(t))B'(t)1 which could be written in differential notation as df(B(t)) = f'(B(t))B'(t) dt = f{B{t))dB{t) However, B (t) is not differentiable, and in particular has nonzero quadratic variation, so the correct formula has an extra term, namely, df(B(t)) = f'(B(t)) dB(t) + \f'{B{t)) ^ . dB(t) dB(t) This is Ito's formula in differential form. Integrating this, we obtain Ito's formula in integral form: f(B(t)) - /(_B(0)) = f f'(B(u)) dB(u) + \ f f"(B(u)) du. 2J0 Remark 15.1 (Differential vs. Integral Forms) The mathematically meaningful form of Ito's formula is Ito's formula in integral form: f(B(t)) - f(B(0)) = f f'(B(u)) dB(u) + \ f f"(B(u)) du. Jo Jo 167

168 This is because we have solid definitions for both integrals appearing on the right-hand side. The first, f f'(B(u))dB(u) Jo is an ltd integral, defined in the previous chapter. The second, f f"(B(u))du, Jo is a Riemann integral, the type used in freshman calculus. For paper and pencil computations, the more convenient form of Ito's rule is Ito's formula in differ- entialform: df(B(t)) = f'(B(t)) dB(t) + \f"{B{t)) dt. There is an intuitive meaning but no solid definition for the terms df(B(t)), dB(t) and dt appearing in this formula. This formula becomes mathematically respectable only after we integrate it. 15.2 Derivation of Ito's formula Consider f(x) = \x2, so that f'(x) = x, f"(x) = l. Let xkl Xk+\ be numbers. Taylor's formula implies f{xk+1) - f(xk) = (xk+1 - xk)f'(xk) + \{xk+l - xk)2f"(xk). In this case, Taylor's formula to second order is exact because / is a quadratic function. In the general case, the above equation is only approximate, and the error is of the order of (xk+i — xk)3. The total error will have limit zero in the last step of the following argument. Fix T > 0 and let II = {t0l t\,... , tn} be a partition of [0, T]. An investor begins with nonrandom initial wealth X0 and at each time t, holds A(i) shares of stock. Stock is modelled by a geometric Brownian motion: dS(t) = fiS(t)dt + aS{t)dB{t).

CHAPTER 15. ltd''s Formula 111 A(t) can be random, but must be adapted. The investor finances his investing by borrowing or lending at interest rate r. Let X (t) denote the wealth of the investor at time t. Then dX(t) = A(t)dS(t) + r [X(t) - A(t)S(t)] dt = A(t) \piS(t)dt + aS{t)dB{t)} + r [X(t) - A(t)S(t)] dt = rX(t)dt + A(t)S(t) (/i-r) dt + A(t)S(t)adB(t). Risk premium Value of an option. Consider an European option which pays g(S(T)) at time T. Letu(i, x) denote the value of this option at time t if the stock price is S(t) = x. In other words, the value of the option at each time t G [0, T] is v(t,S(t)). The differential of this value is dv(t, S(t)) = Vfdt + vxd,S + ^u;E;E(i,S' dS = ujrfi + u^ [fiS dt + (7,5 <i_B] + \vxxo S dt = \vt + fiSvx + ^o-2,S'2u;E;EJ dt + aSvxdB A hedging portfolio starts with some initial wealth X0 and invests so that the wealth X (t) at each time tracks v(t, S{t)). We saw above that dX(t) = [rX + A(/i - r),S] dt + aSAdB. To ensure that X(i) = u(i, ■S'(i)) for all t, we equate coefficients in their differentials. Equating the dB coefficients, we obtain the A-hedging rule: A(t) = vx{t,S(t)). Equating the dt coefficients, we obtain: vt + fj,Svx + \a2S2vxx = rX + A(/i - r)S. But we have set A = vx, and we are seeking to cause X to agree with v. Making these substitutions, we obtain vt + fiSvx + \a2S2vxx = rv + vx((j, - r)S, (where v = v(t,S(t)) and S = S(t)) which simplifies to vt + rSvx + \a2S2vxx = rv. In conclusion, we should let v be the solution to the Black-Scholes partial differential equation vt(t, x) + rxvx(t, x) + \a2x2vxx(t1 x) = rv(t, x) satisfying the terminal condition v{T,x) = g(x). If an investor starts with X0 = u(0, S(0)) and uses the hedge A(i) = f^(i, •S'(i)), then he will have X{t) = v(t, S(t)) for all t, and in particular, X{T) = g{S{T)).

The integrands a(u), /3(w), and Sij(u) can be any adapted processes. The adaptedness of the integrands guarantees that X and Y are also adapted. Chapter 16 Markov processes and the Kolmogorov equations 16.1 Stochastic Differential Equations Consider the stochastic differential equation: dX(t) = a{t,X{t)) dt + a(t,X(t))dB(t). (SDE) Here a(t, x) and a(t, x) are given functions, usually assumed to be continuous in (t, x) and Lips- chitz continuous in a;,i.e., there is a constant L such that \a(t, x) — a(t, y)\ < L\x — y\, |ct(£, x) — a(t, y)\ < L\x — y\ for all t,x,y. Let (t0, x) be given. Theorem 5.51 (Feynman-Kac) Define v(t, x) = Et'xh(X(T)), 0 < t < T, where dX{t) = a{X{t)) dt + a{X{t)) dB{t). Then vt(t, x) + a(x)vx(t, x) + \a2(x)vxx(t1 x) = 0 (FK) and v(T, x) = h(x). The probability measures IP and IP of the Girsanov Theorem are equivalent. Recall that IP is defined by JP(A) = I Z{T) dP, AeT. If IP (A) = 0, then JA Z(T) dP = 0. Chapter 18 Martingale Representation Theorem 18.1 Martingale Representation Theorem See Oksendal, 4th ed., Theorem 4.11, p.50. Theorem 1.56 LetB(t),0 <t < T, be a Brownian motion on (tt, T, P). Let T(t), 0 <t <T,be the filtration generated by this Brownian motion. Let X(t), 0 < t < T, be a martingale (under IP) relative to this filtration. Then there is an adapted process 5(t), 0 < t < T, such that X(t) = X(0) + f S(u) dB(u), 0 < t < T. Jo In particular, the paths ofX are continuous. Remark 18.1 We already know that if X (t) is a process satisfying dX(t) = S(t) dB(t), then X (t) is a martingale. Now we see that if X (t) is a martingale adapted to the filtration generated by the Brownian motion B(t), i.e, the Brownian motion is the only source of randomness in X, then dX(t) = S(t) dB(t) for some S(t). 18.2 A hedging application Homework Problem 4.5. CHAPTER 18. Martingale Representation Theorem 201 Discounted stock prices " (ft)=Mi^wift *+m g "«(" dB'{t) Risk Premium P(') J=i v -y ' dB,{t) For 5.1 to be satisfied, we need to choose 9\(t),... , 6d(t), so that d J2 °ij (t) Oj (t) =tii(t)-r(t), i = l,...,m. (MPR) 3 = 1 Market price of risk. The market price of risk is an adapted process 9(t) = (Q\(t),... , Od(t)) satisfying the system of equations (MPR) above. There are three cases to consider: Case I: (Unique Solution). For Lebesgue-almost every t and ^-almost every u, (MPR) has a unique solution 9(t). Using 9(t) in the rf-dimensional Girsanov Theorem, we define a unique risk-neutral probability measure IP. Under IP, every discounted stock price is a martingale. Consequently, the discounted wealth process corresponding to any portfolio process is a P- martingale, and this implies that the market admits no arbitrage. Finally, the Martingale Representation Theorem can be used to show that every contingent claim can be hedged; the market is said to be complete. Case II: (No solution.) If (MPR) has no solution, then there is no risk-neutral probability measure and the market admits arbitrage. Case III: (Multiple solutions). If (MPR) has multiple solutions, then there are multiple risk-neutral probability measures. The market admits no arbitrage, but there are contingent claims which cannot be hedged; the market is said to be incomplete. Theorem 5.60 (Fundamental Theorem of Asset Pricing) Part I. {Harrison and Pliska, Martingales and Stochastic integrals in the theory of continuous trading, Stochastic Proc. and Applications 11 (1981), pp 215-260.): If a market has a risk-neutral probability measure, then it admits no arbitrage. Part II. (Harrison and Pliska, A stochastic calculus model of continuous trading: complete markets, Stochastic Proc. and Applications 15 (1983), pp 313-316): The risk-neutral measure is unique if and only if every contingent claim can be hedged.

218 v(T, I 0 K L x V(t, x) Syr \ i r • i 0 K L x Figure 20.5: Practial issue. 20.3 A practical issue For t < T but t near T, v(t, x) has the form shown in the bottom part of Fig. 20.5. In particular, the hedging portfolio A(t) = vx(t,S(t)) can become very negative near the knockout boundary. The hedger is in an unstable situation. He should take a large short position in the stock. If the stock does not cross the barrier L, he covers this short position with funds from the money market, pays off the option, and is left with zero. If the stock moves across the barrier, he is now in a region of A(i) = vx(t, S(t)) near zero. He should cover his short position with the money market. This is more expensive than before, because the stock price has risen, and consequently he is left with no money. However, the option has "knocked out", so no money is needed to pay it off. Because a large short position is being taken, a small error in hedging can create a significant effect. Here is a possible resolution. Rather than using the boundary condition v(t,L) = 0, 0 < t < T, solve the PDE with the boundary condition v(t,L) + aLvx(t,L) = 0, 0<t<T, where a is a "tolerance parameter", say 1%. At the boundary, Lvx(t, L) is the dollar size of the short position. The new boundary condition guarantees: 1. Lvx(t, L) remains bounded; 2. The value of the portfolio is always sufficient to cover a hedging error of a times the dollar size of the short position.

Chapter 22 Summary of Arbitrage Pricing Theory A simple European derivative security makes a random payment at a time fixed in advance. The value at time t of such a security is the amount of wealth needed at time t in order to replicate the security by trading in the market. The hedging portfolio is a specification of how to do this trading. 22.1 Binomial model, Hedging Portfolio Let Q be the set of all possible sequences of n coin-tosses. We would like to hedge along every possible path of S (t), but that is impossible. Using the binomial model as a guide, we choose a > 0 and try to hedge along every path S (t) for which the quadratic variation of log S (t) accumulates at rate a2 per unit time. These are the paths with volatility a2. To generate these paths, we use Brownian motion, rather than coin-tossing. To introduce Brownian motion, we need a probability measure. However, the only thing about this probability measure which ultimately matters is the set of paths to which it assigns probability zero.

226 Let B(t), 0 < t < T, be a Brownian motion defined on a probability space (S7, J7, P). For any p <E 1R, the paths of accumulate quadratic variation at rate a2 per unit time. We want to define S(t) = S(0)exp{pt + aB(t)}, so that the paths of log S (t) = log S(0) + pt +aB(t) accumulate quadratic variation at rate a2 per unit time. Surprisingly, the choice of p in this definition is irrelevant. Roughly, the reason for this is the following: Choose u\ G £1. Then, for p\ £ M, ptt + aB^^t), 0 < t < T, is a continuous function of t. If we replace p\ by p2, then p2t + aB(t, u{) is a different function. However, there is an u>2 G £l such that /Oit + CTB(t,a;i) = p2t + aB(t,LJ2), 0<t<T. In other words, regardless of whether we use p\ or p2 in the definition of S (t), we will see the same paths. The mathematically precise statement is the following: If a set of stock price paths has a positive probability when S (t) is defined by S(t) = S(0)exp{Plt + aB(t)}, then this set of paths has positive probability when S (t) is defined by S(t) = S(0)exp{p2t + aB(t)}. Since we are interested in hedging along every path, except possibly for a set of paths which has probability zero, the choice of p is irrelevant. The most convenient choice of p is p = r- \a2, so S(t) = 5(0) exp{r* + aB{t) - \o2t), and e~rtS{t) = S(0) exp{aB(t) - \a2t} is a martingale under IP. Remark 22.1 Although we have taken r and a to be constant, the risk-neutral pricing formula is still "valid" when r and a are processes adapted to the filtration generated by B. If they depend on either B or on S, they are adapted to the filtration generated by B. The "validity" of the risk-neutral pricing formula means: 1. A probability measure is said to be risk-neutral provided

CHAPTER 22. Summary of Arbitrage Pricing Theory 229 • it has the same probability-zero sets as the original measure; • it makes all the discounted asset prices be martingales. Chapter 26 Options on dividend-paying stocks 26.1 American option with convex payoff function Theorem 1.64 Consider the stock price process dS(t) = r(t)S(t) dt + (r(t)S(t) dB(t), where r and a are processes and r(t) > 0, 0 < t < T, a.s. This stock pays no dividends. Let h(x) be a convex function of x > 0, and assume h(0) = 0. (E.g., h(x) = (x — K)+). An American contingent claim paying h(S(t)) if exercised at time t does not need to be exercised before expiration, i.e., waiting until expiration to decide whether to exercise entails no loss of value. One generally has three different instruments: the stock, the money market, and the zero coupon bond. CHAPTER 27. Bonds, forward contracts and futures 269 27.1 Forward contracts We continue with the set-up for zero-coupon bonds. The T-forward price of the stock at time t G [0, T] is the T(t)-measurable price, agreed upon at time t, for purchase of a share of stock at time T, chosen so the forward contract has value zero at time t. Through the device of marking to market, the value of the future contract is maintained at zero at all times. Thus, either party can close out his/her position at any time. The only payment is at time T. With a future contract, entered at time 0, the buyer receives a cash flow (which may at times be negative) between times 0 and T. If he still holds the contract at time T, then he pays S(T) at time T for an asset valued at S (T). The cash flow received between times 0 and T sums to I d$(ti) = $(r)-$(o) = s,(r)-$(o). Jo Thus, if the future contract holder takes delivery at time T, he has paid a total of ($(0) - S{T)) + S(T) = $(0) for an asset valued at S (T). 27.5 Forward-future spread Future price: $(£) = E Forward price: S(T) T(t) Fit) S(t) B{t,T) S(t) (3{t)E P(T) T(t) Forward-future spread: $(0) - F(0) = E[S{T)\ 1 5(0) E \P(T) E E fi (m)Elsm)-E(m If -gjUr and S (T) are uncorrected, /3(X) $(0) = F(0).

CHAPTER 27. Bonds, forward contracts and futures 273 If -gT^r and S (T) are positively correlated, then $(0) <F(0). This is the case that a rise in stock price tends to occur with a fall in the interest rate. The owner of the future tends to receive income when the stock price rises, but invests it at a declining interest rate. If the stock price falls, the owner usually must make payments on the future contract. He withdraws from the money market to do this just as the interest rate rises. In short, the long position in the future is hurt by positive correlation between t^t and S (T). The buyer of the future is compensated by a reduction of the future price below the forward price. 27.6 Backwardation and contango Suppose dS(t) = fiS(t) dt + aS{t) dW{t). Chapter 28 Term-structure models Throughout this discussion, {W{t); 0 < t < T*} is a Brownian motion on some probability space (0, T, P), and {Fit); 0 < t < F*} is the filtration generated by W. Suppose we are given an adapted interest rate process {r(i); 0 < t < F*}. We define the accumulation factor fi(t) = exp [ / r(u) du\ , 0 < t < F*. In a term-structure model, we take the zero-coupon bonds ("zeroes") of various maturities to be the primitive assets. We assume these bonds are default-free and pay $1 at maturity. For 0 < t < F < T*,let B(t, F) = price at time t of the zero-coupon bond paying $1 at time F. Theorem 0.67 (Fundamental Theorem of Asset Pricing) A term structure model is free of arbitrage if and only if there is a probability measure IP on Q (a risk-neutral measure) with the same probability-zero sets as IP (i.e., equivalent to IP), such that for each F £ (0, F*], the process B(t,T) ———- 0 < t < F is a martingale under IP. Remark 28.1 We shall always have dB(t,T) = fj,(t,T)B(t,T) dt + p(t,T)B(t,T) dW(t), 0 < t < F, for some functions /i(£, T) and p(t,T). Therefore K^w)=B{t^diw))+W)dmT) = [pit,F) - r(t)]^p dt + p(t,T)^p dWit), 275

276 so IP is a risk-neutral measure if and only if/i(i, T), the mean rate of return of B(t, T) under IP, is the interest rate r (t). If the mean rate of return of B (£, T) under _P is not r (t) at each time t and for each maturity T, we should change to a measure _P under which the mean rate of return is r(t). If such a measure does not exist, then the model admits an arbitrage by trading in zero-coupon bonds. 28.1 Computing arbitrage-free bond prices: first method Begin with a stochastic differential equation (SDE) dX(t) = a(t,X(t)) dt + b(t,X(t)) dW{t). The solution X(t) is the factor. If we want to have n-factors, we let W be an ?i-dimensional Brownian motion and let X be an ?i-dimensional process. We let the interest rate r(t) be a function of X(t). In the usual one-factor models, we take r(t) to be X(t) (e.g., Cox-Ingersoll-Ross, Hull- White). Now that we have an interest rate process {r(i); 0 < t < T*}, we define the zero-coupon bond prices to be B(t,T) = /3(t) E E exp ■ fi{T) T{t) r(u) du T{t) 0 < t < T < T*. Remark 28.3 It is customary in the literature to write W rather than W and IP rather than IP, so that IP is the symbol used for the risk-neutral measure and no reference is ever made to the market measure. The only parameter which must be estimated from the market is the bond volatility a* (i, T), and volatility is unaffected by the change of measure.

The means are random through a dependence on r(t). Advantages of the Hull & White model: 1. Leads to closed-form pricing formulas. 2. Allows calibration to fit initial yield curve exactly. Short-comings of the Hull & White model: 1. One-factor, so only allows parallel shifts of the yield curve, i.e., B(t, T) = exp {-r(t)C(t, T) - A(t, T)} , so bond prices of all maturities are perfectly correlated. 2. Interest rate is normally distributed, and hence can take negative values. Consequently, the bond price B(t,T) = E exp r(u) du T{t) can exceed 1.

Chapter 33 Change of numeraire Consider a Brownian motion driven market model with time horizon T*. For now, we will have one asset, which we call a "stock" even though in applications it will usually be an interest rate dependent claim. The price of the stock is modeled by dS(t) = r(t) S(t) dt + <i(t)S(t) dW(t), (0.1) where the interest rate process r (t) and the volatility process a (t) are adapted to some filtration {T(t); 0 < t < T*}. W is a Brownian motion relative to this filtration, but {T(t); 0 < t < T*} may be larger than the filtration generated by W. This is not a geometric Brownian motion model. We are particularly interested in the case that the interest rate is stochastic, given by a term structure model we have not yet specified. We shall work only under the risk-neutral measure, which is reflected by the fact that the mean rate of return for the stock is r (t). We then denominate all other assets in units of this numeraire. Example 33.1 (Money market as numeraire) Tlie money market could be the numeraire. At time t, the stock is worth '-AA units of money market and the T-maturity bond is worth \,\ ' units of money market. Example 33.2 (Bond as numeraire) Tlie T-maturity bond could be the numeraire. At time t <T, the stock is worth F(t,T) units of T-maturity bond and the T-maturity bond is worth 1 unit. ■ We will say that a probability measure En is risk-neutral for the numeraire N if every asset price, divided by N, is a martingale under E^. The original probability measure E is risk-neutral for the numeraire fi (Example 33.1).
